Let me carefully work through this cross-examination brief on row O5 (Greta's dough). My job: try to overturn the determination's ruling that file 10 gives AGREE and file 11 gives AGREE on O5, verdict SAME, passage CHANGED (additive) through f11 L161 s3 and L514. The question is whether file 11 reaches the fixed verdict on every point (AGREE), or leaves a point open (SILENT) or supports two readings (SPLIT).

Let me set up the facts.

**The case O5 - Greta's dough:**
- Situation: Greta's rule says bread dough doubles in two hours. It fails in January. She restates it for rooms warmer than twenty degrees, and her account says why: the yeast that makes the gas works slowly in the cold. Replace her limit with "on days other than Tuesdays" and her account has nothing to say about why Tuesdays would matter.
- Thoughtful person's verdict (fixed verdict): "This is a legitimate narrowing. The limit follows from a part of her account."

**Marks:** File 10: 1K A AGREE, 1K B AGREE (baseline AGREE). File 11: 1C A SILENT, 1C B SILENT; own SILENT, AGREE, SILENT; blind SILENT, AGREE, SILENT. So 4 of 5 file-11 readings are SILENT (only auditor 1 on B reached AGREE, via a route the determiner rejects).

**The determination's ruling:** file 10 AGREE (baseline); file 11 AGREE; verdict SAME; passage CHANGED (additive), f11 L161 s3 and L514; no change. Confidence medium.

**The determiner's reasons for AGREE under file 11:**
- f11 L161 s3 makes appropriateness "part of the claim", and f11 L514 makes it a "stated input" on which the verdict is "a verdict given the input".
- Greta's claim states it: "her account says why: the yeast that makes the gas works slowly in the cold." The input is present. The verdict given it is that the restriction is appropriate, because the limit follows from a part of her account. That is the fixed verdict's finding, qualified as given the stated ground and open to criticism. A qualification is the same finding (brief, "Same finding").
- "Supplies no rule that certifies it" says the semantics adds no rule of its own. It does not withhold the verdict given the stated ground, which L514 grants in terms.
- If O5 were SILENT, L514's "a verdict given the input" would never yield a verdict on appropriateness, and its contrast "where the input is missing" would do no work for Part III.
- O1 is the contrast: its input is missing, and it is SILENT.

**The contrary readings:**
- 1C A, SILENT: "The quoted sentences leave the legitimacy of a restriction to a criticizable input with no certifying rule, so the theory stops short of the fixed verdict's finding." Rejected its own AGREE reading "because the same sentence says the semantics 'supplies no rule that certifies it', so the endorsement is the reader's, not the theory's."
- 1C B, SILENT: "The theory lists what makes a restriction appropriate among its declared inputs and certifies none, so the legitimacy of this narrowing is left open." Rejected AGREE on the stated-scope sentence (f11 L259 = f10 L272) "because that sentence requires only that a restriction be stated, which 'Tuesdays' would satisfy too, so it cannot tell the legitimate limit from the arbitrary one."

**What turns on it:** If the contrary held, O5 would be AGREE→SILENT, a theory change away, test (b) fails.

Now, my job as cross-examiner: try to show from the texts that the ruling is wrong: file11 should be SILENT (or SPLIT), or the file 10 mark is wrong, or the verdict/passage/direction/kind is wrong.

## Scope, and a question that can be wrong

A contract is a declared subset of the physically admitted changes, and a stated scope is what makes it one. An account at a stated scope answers the question asked at that scope; it does not answer a broader question that failed, and a narrowing adopted after a failure is a new claim at a new index (Part VIII). What makes a restriction appropriate to the question asked is a substantive, criticizable part of the claim; the semantics records the restriction and supplies no rule that certifies it. [L161]
```

```
**Declared inputs.** Besides the two primitives, some claims take stated inputs that the semantics records and does not supply: the obligations \(O\) and \(P\) of a repair, with the occasions each covers (Part XI); the scope of a contract and what makes a restriction appropriate (Part III); the system boundary and continuity of an attribution (Part XII). So L514 = "**Declared inputs.** Besides the two primitives, some claims take stated inputs... [the scope of a contract and what makes a restriction appropriate (Part III)] ... Now the marks rules (Stage 1 brief):
- **The theory's verdict.** What the theory, read as it stands, finds on each point, by its own definitions and conditions. Where the theory leaves a word undefined, read it in its ordinary sense.
- **Same finding.** A verdict of the theory that reaches the fixed verdict's finding and adds a qualification, a limit or a more exact wording reaches the same finding. Write the addition under NOTE. A mark records findings; differences of wording belong in NOTE.
- **SILENT**: on at least one point, the theory leaves the finding open, because it takes the deciding matter as an input the case leaves unstated, or because its sentences stop short of deciding it. Name the point and record your search: the Parts you read for it and the words you looked for.

The fixed verdict's points:
1. "This is a legitimate narrowing." — i.e., the narrowing to "rooms warmer than twenty degrees" is legitimate.
2. "The limit follows from a part of her account." — the limit follows from a part of the account (the yeast component).

So the theory must find, on the point "the restriction to >20°C is legitimate/appropriate," a verdict. And on the point "the limit follows from a part of her account."

Now the key question: does file 11's theory reach the finding "this is a legitimate narrowing" or does it leave it open?

The determiner says: f11 L161 s3 + L514 mean that when the input (what makes the restriction appropriate) is stated, the theory gives "a verdict given the input": the verdict is that the restriction is appropriate, given the stated ground. Greta's case states the ground ("her account says why: the yeast... works slowly in the cold"). Hence AGREE with a qualification (given the stated ground, open to criticism).

The contrary: the theory "supplies no rule that certifies it" and "certifies none", so the legitimacy is left open → SILENT. And the SILENT rule: "because its sentences stop short of deciding it."

L161 s3: "What makes a restriction appropriate to the question asked is a substantive, criticizable part of the claim; the semantics records the restriction and supplies no rule that certifies it."

This says: (i) appropriateness is a substantive, criticizable part of the claim — i.e., the claim itself includes what makes the restriction appropriate; (ii) the semantics records the restriction and supplies no certifying rule.

L514: "some claims take stated inputs that the semantics records and does not supply: ... the scope of a contract and what makes a restriction appropriate (Part III); ... A verdict that depends on one of these is a verdict given the input; where the input is missing, the verdict is unsettled and the semantics says so rather than choosing the input from the verdict wanted."

So what makes a restriction appropriate is a *declared input*, i.e., something the semantics "records and does not supply." A verdict that depends on it is "a verdict given the input." Where the input is missing, "the verdict is unsettled."

Now the question is: given Greta's case, is the input present or missing? The determiner says the input is present: "her account says why: the yeast that makes the gas works slowly in the cold."

Hmm — but is that the input "what makes a restriction appropriate"? Or is the input merely *asserted* as appropriate? Let's parse:

The semantics does not supply a rule certifying appropriateness. So the theory's own definitions and conditions... do they find the narrowing legitimate? The theory says: when a claim states what makes the restriction appropriate, the verdict that depends on it is "a verdict given the input." So the theory would say: "given the input (the yeast account makes the >20° restriction appropriate), the restriction is appropriate."

The SILENT mark rule: "the theory leaves the finding open, because it takes the deciding matter as an input the case leaves unstated, or because its sentences stop short of deciding it."

Two routes to SILENT: (a) the deciding matter is an input the case leaves unstated; (b) the theory's sentences stop short of deciding it.

Here the case does state something: "her account says why: the yeast that makes the gas works slowly in the cold." But is that the *input* ("what makes a restriction appropriate") or is it a *candidate ground* whose adequacy the theory does not assess?

The key tension: L161 s3 says the semantics "supplies no rule that certifies it." So even with the ground stated, the theory does not certify the restriction as appropriate. The theory *records* the restriction and the claimed ground. Whether the ground *makes* the restriction appropriate is... a criticizable part of the claim, i.e., something that can be attacked, but the semantics supplies no rule that decides it in either direction.

**Sharper analysis of "a verdict given the input":**

L514 s4-ish: "A verdict that depends on one of these is a verdict given the input; where the input is missing, the verdict is unsettled and the semantics says so rather than choosing the input from the verdict wanted."

Parse: "A verdict that depends on one of these [declared inputs] is a verdict given the input." This is a *characterization* of verdicts: they are relativized. "Where the input is missing, the verdict is unsettled." So verdicts come in two states: given the input (when present), unsettled (when missing). The word "verdict" here is used for the outcome of applying the semantics to a claim. So on a point whose deciding matter is a declared input:
- input present → the semantics yields "a verdict given the input" — i.e., the verdict holds *relative to* the input.
- input missing → unsettled.

So if Greta's claim supplies "what makes a restriction appropriate" (the yeast ground), the semantics yields a verdict *given that input*. Which verdict? The one that "depends on" the input. The point at issue — "this is a legitimate narrowing" — depends on what makes the restriction appropriate. So the semantics' verdict on that point, given the input, would be... the verdict that the input delivers. The input is: "the limit follows from a part of her account." Given that input, the narrowing is legitimate? Hmm — but "given the input" means the verdict is conditional on the input being as stated. If the input is "the yeast ground is what makes the restriction appropriate," then given the input, the restriction is appropriate.

L161 s3: "What makes a restriction appropriate to the question asked is a substantive, criticizable part of the claim" — i.e., the *claim* is supposed to contain what makes the restriction appropriate. Greta's claim does say why — the account says why the limit is at 20°. So arguably the claim does contain "what makes a restriction appropriate."

Actually, let me reconsider whether L161 s3 and L514 really harmonize into SILENT.

L161 s3: "What makes a restriction appropriate to the question asked is a substantive, criticizable part of the claim; the semantics records the restriction and supplies no rule that certifies it."

Key: "is a substantive, criticizable part of the claim." This *identifies* where appropriateness lives: in the claim, as a substantive part. The claim's part is "the yeast that makes the gas works slowly in the cold" — a substantive part of her account. "Criticizable" = can be attacked (e.g., someone could argue the yeast account is wrong). "The semantics records the restriction and supplies no rule that certifies it" = the semantics doesn't add a certification rule.

So under L161 s3 alone: the claim contains what makes the restriction appropriate; the semantics doesn't certify. Now, what is the theory's *verdict* on the case's point? Hmm, but wait: "the semantics records the restriction and supplies no rule that certifies it" — under this reading, the first clause of s3 provides the criterion and the second clause says the semantics doesn't certify. Ugh. "Certify" — to certify is to attest/guarantee as meeting a standard. "The semantics supplies no rule that certifies it" = the semantics gives no rule that would *establish* that a restriction is appropriate (independently of the claim's assertion). So appropriateness is always *claimed*, never *certified by the semantics*. But then, is the theory's verdict "legitimate (claimed)" or "undecided"?

Compare: the marks rule for the theory's verdict — "What the theory, read as it stands, finds on each point, by its own definitions and conditions." And "criticizable" is a qualification.

Or is it a statement about *where the question of appropriateness lives* (in the claim, as a substantive part — i.e., the claim must carry its own justification — as opposed to being settled by the semantics)? Hmm. "What makes a restriction appropriate... is a substantive, criticizable part of the claim" — I think this says: the thing that makes a restriction appropriate is [found in] a substantive, criticizable part of the claim. I.e., appropriateness is grounded in the claim's content, and that content is criticizable. So: if the claim has such a part, the restriction is appropriate *by the claim's lights*; the semantics records this and doesn't certify it (doesn't independently verify that the part really makes it appropriate).

Hmm, so the theory's finding is: "appropriate, in virtue of the claim's substantive part, as claimed, uncertified." Is that "the same finding" as "this is a legitimate narrowing"? The first half — "legitimate" — is matched only if "appropriate by the claim's substantive part, uncertified" counts as "legitimate" with a qualification.

Hmm, hmm, hmm. The Same finding rule: "A verdict of the theory that reaches the fixed verdict's finding and adds a qualification, a limit or a more exact wording reaches the same finding."

"Reaches the fixed verdict's finding and adds a qualification" — does "appropriate as claimed, uncertified and criticizable" *reach* "legitimate"? If the theory's verdict is "the narrowing is appropriate in virtue of the account's part (though uncertified)," that reaches "legitimate" and adds the limit "uncertified, criticizable" → AGREE. If the theory's verdict is "whether it is appropriate is a matter of criticism the semantics doesn't decide," that doesn't reach "legitimate" → SILENT.

So everything turns on: does the theory *find* appropriateness (via its criterion "a substantive part of the claim") or does it *decline to find* (via "supplies no rule that certifies it")?

The determiner: L514's "a verdict given the input" shows the theory does deliver verdicts on inputs when stated. The readers: "supplies no rule that certifies it" and "certifies none" show the theory declines.

Let me look at L514 once more very carefully:

"**Declared inputs.** Besides the two primitives, some claims take stated inputs that the semantics records and does not supply: the obligations O and P of a repair, with the occasions each covers (Part XI); the scope of a contract and what makes a restriction appropriate (Part III); the system boundary and continuity of an attribution (Part XII). A verdict that depends on one of these is a verdict given the input; where the input is missing, the verdict is unsettled and the semantics says so rather than choosing the input from the verdict wanted."

OK so "what makes a restriction appropriate" is a *stated input* — "the semantics records and does not supply" it. This is crucial: *what makes a restriction appropriate* is an INPUT. It is not something the semantics finds or derives. It is something the *claim* states and the semantics records.

So in Greta's case, the input = what makes the >20° restriction appropriate = "the yeast works slowly in the cold" (stated by her account). The semantics records it and does not supply it.

"A verdict that depends on one of these is a verdict given the input." So verdicts that depend on this input are "given the input."

Now: what verdict depends on the input "what makes the restriction appropriate"? A verdict like "the narrowing is legitimate" depends on it. So it becomes "the narrowing is legitimate *given the input*." Hmm! And "where the input is missing, the verdict is unsettled" — Tuesday case: input missing → unsettled.

But here's the catch: if "what makes a restriction appropriate" is an input, then the verdict "the narrowing is legitimate" is *given the input* — meaning: *assuming the input* (that the yeast ground is what makes it appropriate), the narrowing is legitimate. But is "the narrowing is legitimate given the input" a verdict the theory *reaches*? The theory says such verdicts are "given the input" — it relativizes them. It doesn't *establish* them; the input is assumed.

Hmm, and what does the mark "AGREE" require? "on every point, the theory's verdict reaches the same finding as the fixed verdict." The fixed verdict's finding is unqualified: "This is a legitimate narrowing." The theory's verdict: "legitimate given the stated ground (uncertified, criticizable)." Under Same finding, a qualification or limit is allowed. "Given the stated ground and uncertified" is a limit. So AGREE?

Versus SILENT: "the theory leaves the finding open, because it takes the deciding matter as an input the case leaves unstated, or because its sentences stop short of deciding it."

"it takes the deciding matter as an input" — the theory *does* take the deciding matter (what makes a restriction appropriate) as an input! That's literally what L514 says. The second disjunct of the SILENT trigger — "the case leaves unstated" — hmm, the case *states* the ground. So the first SILENT route requires the case to leave the input unstated. The case states it. Hmm.

"her account says why" — why the limit is at twenty degrees. So the account contains a ground. So the theory's verdict on "is the narrowing legitimate?" is: the theory has no condition that decides it; it records the claim's stated ground as a criticizable part and certifies nothing.

Compare with a clear SILENT case's structure: "the theory leaves the finding open, because it takes the deciding matter as an input the case leaves unstated, or because its sentences stop short of deciding it." But then the SILENT trigger's first route fails ("the case leaves unstated") and we need the second route ("its sentences stop short of deciding it"). Do the theory's sentences stop short of deciding legitimacy? YES if "supplies no rule that certifies it" means what it says: no rule decides appropriateness. The theory's sentences describe where appropriateness lives and how verdicts are input-relative; none of them *decides* whether a given restriction is legitimate. I.e., the semantics *accepts the claim's stated input* and delivers the verdict conditional on it. "Given the input" = conditional on the input. So the verdict is: "appropriate, given the stated ground."

I think both readings of "a verdict given the input" are textually available:
(R1) The semantics delivers conditional verdicts: "appropriate given the input." → reaches P1 with a limit → AGREE.
(R2) The semantics relativizes whatever verdicts it has; on appropriateness it has none (no certifying rule), so P1 is left open → SILENT.

For Same finding to apply, the theory's verdict must *reach the finding* (legitimate) and *add* a qualification. If the theory's verdict is "uncertified, left to criticism," it hasn't reached "legitimate" and added a limit — it has replaced the finding with a disclaimer. What does the theory *find* on "is this narrowing legitimate?" — I.e., applying its definitions/conditions to the case: the narrowing is stated ✓ (L259); the claim contains what it says makes the restriction appropriate (the yeast part) ✓ (L161 s3 c1: "a substantive... part of the claim" is present); the semantics certifies nothing (L161 s3 c2). So the theory's finding: "the restriction's appropriateness rests on the claim's substantive part [present]; uncertified by the semantics; open to criticism."

Does that *reach* "This is a legitimate narrowing"? The theory's finding says the appropriateness *rests on the claim's part* and is *uncertified*. Hmm. "Rests on a part of the claim" ≈ P2. "Uncertified" ≈ the theory declines P1.

For the mark: is the theory's finding on P1 the *same finding* as "legitimate" with a limit, or does it leave P1 open?

The key word in "Same finding": "A verdict of the theory that **reaches** the fixed verdict's finding and **adds** a qualification, a limit or a more exact wording reaches the same finding." — the theory's verdict must *reach* the finding. "Uncertified" is not "reaches and adds a limit"; "uncertified" negates the reaching. If I say "John is tall, though I can't certify it," I've reached "John is tall" with a limit. If I say "John's tallness rests on his own claim; I certify nothing," I've *not* reached "John is tall."

Hmm! But "The restriction's appropriateness rests on the claim's substantive part (present)" — that IS reaching appropriateness, with the source attributed to the claim. Hmm. "Appropriate in virtue of the account's part" — the fixed verdict says "This is a legitimate narrowing. The limit follows from a part of her account." Hmm, the fixed verdict *itself* grounds legitimacy in "the limit follows from a part of her account"!! P1 (legitimate) is grounded by P2 (limit follows from account part). So the thoughtful person's *own* logic: legitimate *because* the limit follows from a part of her account. And the theory's finding: the restriction's appropriateness is a substantive part of the claim [the account says why: the yeast] — i.e., the limit's appropriateness *is* a part of her account. So the theory's finding *is* P2-as-ground, and P1 is the verdict the thoughtful person draws from it.

So: does the theory draw P1 from P2? The thoughtful person draws it ("This is a legitimate narrowing. The limit follows from a part of her account"). The theory says: appropriateness *is* the claim's part (P2's structure); the semantics doesn't certify. Hmm.

Now let me stress-test the SILENT ruling against the determiner's five reasons (I must "give its reasons their full weight"):

R1: "f11 L161 s3 makes appropriateness 'part of the claim', and f11 L514 makes it a 'stated input' on which the verdict is 'a verdict given the input'."

So on the *worth* of the obligations, the semantics issues NO verdict — even with the input stated! It means: *verdicts the semantics issues* that depend on inputs are input-tagged. For O,P: repair verdicts (P) — issued, tagged. For boundary/continuity: capability verdicts — issued, tagged. For scope+appropriateness: verdicts at an index — issued, tagged. (iii) The determiner's reductio proves too much: on the same argument, the clause would have to yield *worth* verdicts on O and P, which Part XI forbids in terms.

Great. Now the report structure:

Points (most serious first):

1. **The core point: file 11's sentences stop short of the finding "this is a legitimate narrowing" (SILENT, point P1).** The theory's only statements on restriction-appropriateness are f11 L161 s3 ("the semantics records the restriction and supplies no rule that certifies it") and f11 L514 ("stated inputs that the semantics records and does not supply: … what makes a restriction appropriate (Part III) … A verdict that depends on one of these is a verdict given the input; where the input is missing, the verdict is unsettled …"). Both say the semantics neither supplies nor certifies appropriateness. The fixed verdict's P1 *is* a certification ("This is a legitimate narrowing"). The theory's verdict on P1 is an abstention — "recorded, uncertified, criticizable" — which stops short. SILENT rule, second limb.

2. **The reading that would produce "appropriate given the input" needs a decision rule the text expressly withholds.** The determination's step "The verdict given it is that the restriction is appropriate" has no source: L514's "a verdict given the input" is a *status* for verdicts the semantics issues, not a generator. If f11 L161 s3's first clause ("What makes a restriction appropriate … is a substantive, criticizable part of the claim") were the decision rule (claim-has-ground → legitimate), it would be exactly "a rule that certifies it," which the same sentence denies — and it would be the semantics "supplying" what makes a restriction appropriate, which L514 denies ("records and does not supply"). The clause must be placement: the ground lives in the claim and is criticizable. [The criterion/placement + contradiction point.]

3. **The Part XI parallel: declared inputs are used, never evaluated.** "Their declaration makes no claim that the aims are worth pursuing, and (P) does not rank alternatives" (Part XI, Repair paragraph) — the semantics issues verdicts *given* O and P but never on their merits. Same design here. So L514's "verdict given the input" cannot be an appropriateness finding. This answers the determiner's "would do no work" reason: the clause governs the verdicts the semantics *does* issue that depend on the scope item (e.g., f11 L161 s2's "an account at a stated scope answers the question asked at that scope") and forbids back-filling ("rather than choosing the input from the verdict wanted"); the same argument would force *worth* verdicts on O and P, which Part XI forbids.

4. **The "Same finding" rule is applied without its first condition.** The rule requires "A verdict of the theory that **reaches** the fixed verdict's finding **and adds** a qualification." The determination's reason paraphrases it as "A qualification is the same finding" — dropping "reaches." "Given the stated ground and open to criticism" is a *disclaimer* about the standing of a finding, not a limit on a finding reached. On P2 (descriptive: "the limit follows from a part of her account") the record matches the finding; on P1 (evaluative) it abstains.

5. **The only operative scope condition cannot draw the case's contrast.** f11 L259 (= f10 L272) requires only that exclusions be "excluded by a stated scope, not silently." The Tuesday limit, once stated, satisfies it too — 1C B's point, unanswered in the determination. No definition or condition of the theory (mark rule: "by its own definitions and conditions") separates the legitimate narrowing from the arbitrary one, so none can find the former legitimate.

6. **The case's own Tuesday counterfactual refutes the "given the input" pairing as a legitimacy verdict.** Under the determination's reading, the Tuesday variant ("her account has nothing to say about why Tuesdays would matter") yields "the verdict is unsettled" — not "illegitimate." A machinery that can never find a narrowing illegitimate is not finding "legitimate" in the thoughtful person's contrastive sense; it is abstaining in both directions. O1's SILENT is consistent with this reading.

**1. The file-11 mark must be SILENT on the first point of the fixed verdict, "This is a legitimate narrowing."** That point is an evaluation of the restriction. File 11 speaks to such evaluations twice, and both times withholds them. f11 L161 s3: "What makes a restriction appropriate to the question asked is a substantive, criticizable part of the claim; the semantics records the restriction and supplies no rule that certifies it." f11 L514: "some claims take stated inputs that the semantics records and does not supply: … the scope of a contract and what makes a restriction appropriate (Part III) … A verdict that depends on one of these is a verdict given the input; where the input is missing, the verdict is unsettled and the semantics says so rather than choosing the input from the verdict wanted." What makes a restriction appropriate is thus an input "the semantics records and does not supply," and the semantics "supplies no rule that certifies" a restriction. The mark rules ask what the theory "finds on each point, by its own definitions and conditions," and SILENT applies where "its sentences stop short of deciding it." On "is this narrowing legitimate?" the theory's sentences record the claim's ground and abstain. That is stopping short. (SILENT precedes AGREE in the rules' order, so if SILENT fits, AGREE is not reached.) The second point, "The limit follows from a part of her account," is descriptive and is at best *recorded* as the claim's stated ground ("a substantive … part of the claim"); one point left open suffices for SILENT.

**2. The ruling's bridge — "The verdict given it is that the restriction is appropriate" — has no source in f11 L514.** L514 classifies verdicts ("A verdict that depends on one of these is a verdict given the input"); it does not manufacture one. The verdict must come from the theory's definitions and conditions; on restriction-appropriateness there is none (point 3). The clause is the semantics' honesty discipline over the verdicts it does issue, and Part XI shows the design exactly: of the declared inputs O and P the theory says "Their declaration makes no claim that the aims are worth pursuing, and (P) does not rank alternatives" (Part XI, Repair) — it issues repair verdicts given O and P and never a verdict on their merits. Restriction-appropriateness is the same kind of item. This also answers the ruling's reductio ("would do no work"): the clause governs the scope-dependent verdicts the semantics does issue — e.g. f11 L161 s2's "An account at a stated scope answers the question asked at that scope," which is a verdict given the stated scope — and it forbids back-filling ("rather than choosing the input from the verdict wanted"). On the ruling's reading the same clause would have to yield *worth* verdicts on O and P, which Part XI forbids in terms.

**3. To read "appropriate given the input" out of f11 L161 s3's first clause, the clause has to be a decision rule — and the same sentence and L514 both deny that the semantics has one.** If "What makes a restriction appropriate … is a substantive, criticizable part of the claim" meant "a restriction is appropriate when the claim has such a part," then the semantics would be supplying exactly "a rule that certifies it," which the next clause denies ("supplies no rule that certifies it"), and would be *supplying* "what makes a restriction appropriate," which L514 denies ("records and does not supply"). The clause is therefore placement: the ground lives in the claim and is open to criticism; the semantics records it. With no decision rule, there is no verdict for L514's "given the input" to qualify, and no certification to hand to the "Same finding" rule.

**4. "Same finding" is applied without its first condition.** The rule reads: "A verdict of the theory that reaches the fixed verdict's finding *and adds* a qualification, a limit or a more exact wording reaches the same finding." The ruling's reason compresses this to "A qualification is the same finding," and then supplies only a qualification — "given the stated ground and open to criticism." But "recorded, uncertified, criticizable" is not a limit on a finding reached; it is a statement that the finding is not established. The fixed verdict asserts "This is a legitimate narrowing"; the theory's answer is that appropriateness is a criticizable claim-part the semantics certifies nothing about. That is the abstention SILENT records, not AGREE-with-NOTE.

**5. The only operative scope condition cannot draw the distinction the case draws.** f11 L259 (word for word f10 L272) requires only "that every physically admitted edit excluded from \(C\) is excluded by a stated scope, not silently." "On days other than Tuesdays," once stated, satisfies that too — 1C B's point, which the ruling does not answer. No definition or condition of the theory separates Greta's limit from the Tuesday limit, so none can find the first legitimate. Auditor 1's AGREE route (non-circular dependence, f11 L257) fails for the reason the ruling gives — "A Tuesday limit would leave the yeast block removable too" — and with it goes the last condition that might have carried AGREE. What remains is the input-status prose of points 1–3, which withholds the verdict.

**6. The case's own counterfactual shows the "given the input" pairing is an abstention in both directions.** The ruling's contrast reason ("O1 is the contrast: its input is missing, and it is SILENT") treats present-input as the positive half of a pair. But on the case's Tuesday variant the same pairing gives "the verdict is unsettled" — not "illegitimate," which is the thoughtful person's contrast ("her account has nothing to say about why Tuesdays would matter"). A machinery that can never find a narrowing illegitimate is not finding "legitimate" here; it is abstaining whenever the question is the legitimacy of the restriction itself.
